chapter 14.
Benchmarking: Predicting Real Experience, Not 
a Leaderboard
The Architect’s Question
After this chapter we should be able to look at a benchmark result and judge whether it predicts 
what our users will actually experience. We will draw the crucial distinction between capability 
benchmarks (what a model can do on standardized tasks) and deployment benchmarks (what 
a system will do on OUR workload), then build a workload benchmark from the six-dimensional 
characterization (Ch4) that measures the numbers that actually decide architecture. After this chapter, 
“the model scores well on MMLU” is recognized as a weak claim — and “it meets our p95-TTFT SLO 
at 40 rps on our token profile” is a strong one.
14.1. Concept
Benchmarking fails most often because the wrong benchmark is used for the wrong decision. The fix 
is to separate two very different instruments:
1. Capability benchmarks — measure the model’s output quality on standardized tasks (MMLU, 
GSM8K, HumanEval, MT-Bench, retrieval-QA suites). They answer: is this model good enough 
at the skill our workload needs? They do NOT tell us how fast, how cheap, or how it behaves 
under our traffic — model capability is the goal (Ch4’s Quality dimension), never the bottleneck.
2. Deployment benchmarks — measure the system’s behavior on a representative workload: 
TTFT, TPOT, throughput, goodput, KV cache utilization, cost per request. They answer: does 
169

Benchmarking: Predicting Real Experience, Not a Leaderboard
170
this model-on-this-hardware meet the SLO at our traffic and token profile? These are the numbers 
that actually pick the architecture.
The core insight: a capability leaderboard is a poor proxy for serving performance. Two 
models with identical MMLU can differ wildly in TTFT under the same batching, because serving 
depends on tokenizer, context length, KV layout, and scheduler — none of which a leaderboard scores. 
Benchmarks select models only after capability filters and deployment measurement both pass.
14.2. Mental Model
Capability benchmarks serve as screening and deployment benchmarks as selection. Screening 
answers “which models are even in the running?” cheaply and broadly. Selection answers “among 
the screened-in models, which system actually works for our workload?” expensively and precisely. 
The two-stage mental model prevents two classic errors:
• Screening with deployment metrics — e.g. benchmarking a model’s MMLU to pick a serving 
stack. (Wrong tool.)
• Selecting with leaderboards — e.g. taking the top-scoring MMLU model and assuming it will 
meet our SLO. (Wrong tool, again.)
Just as important, we benchmark the workload, not the model in the abstract. A deployment 
benchmark that doesn’t reproduce our token profile (input/output ratio, context length), our traffic 
(rps, concurrency, burst), and our SLO (percentiles) is measuring someone else’s problem.
14.3. Worked Example
14.3.1. Designing a Deployment Benchmark for the Canonical Workload
We build a deployment benchmark for the canonical enterprise-Q&A RAG workload (canonical 
scenario (Ch 4, Table 4-2)): ~10 rps average / 40 rps peak, ~9,200 input tokens (1,200 query + 8K 
retrieved) + ~300 output, TTFT budget (median ≤ 1.2 s, p95 ≤ 2 s), TPOT ~25 ms/token. [canonical 
scenario (Ch 4, Table 4-2)]
Step 1 — representative queries. We sample real user questions and run them through the rag 
pipeline to produce the actual prompt shapes, matching the ~9.2K-in / ~300-out token profile with 
the model’s own tokenizer (not a heuristic). [2°]
Step 2 — sweep the offered load, do NOT test a single point. The analytical concurrency figure 
(C ≈ 17.5, ~2.0 req/s) is a starting hypothesis, not the answer. We sweep the offered load/concurrency 
across a range and measure the tail at each point, so the benchmark finds the SLO-satisfying operating 
regime instead of validating the model at the point the model selected. Run a load series (e.g. 2, 4, 
8, 12, 16, 20, 24, 28 concurrent at the canonical 9.2K-in/300-out profile, scaled to a host’s achievable 
concurrency) and record, at each level:

171
Benchmarking: Predicting Real Experience, Not a Leaderboard
• TTFT p50/p95/p99 (must be median ≤ 1.2 s, p95 ≤ 2 s).
• TPOT p50/p95 (≤ ~25 ms median).
• Goodput — tokens/s that meet the SLO (Ch6), not raw throughput.
• KV cache utilization and prefix-cache hit ratio (to validate the serving choices of Ch11).
• Admission / rejection — the offered-load level at which the server starts queueing or rejecting 
(the saturation knee).
The analytical ceiling sets the range of the sweep and flags which levels are physically impossible 
(a single 8×H100 host cannot hold ~344 in-flight requests; that is a fleet burst served by ~20 hosts, 
Ch 16-17). The benchmark then reads off the highest offered load that still meets the SLO — that 
measured SLO-goodput is the provisioning input, not the analytical C/W. The server is loaded at the 
concurrency it can actually hold, and we sweep across that achievable range rather than testing one 
number.
Step 3 — read the result. Suppose candidate A (a 70B dense on 8×H100) shows p95 TTFT 1.9 s 
and a measured goodput that meets the per-request TTFT SLO at the ~17.5-concurrent / ~2.0-req-per-
second analytical load it can hold. The host’s prefill ceiling from Ch 8 is ~19,800 tok/s — the right 
order for the prefill leg of that rate (2.0 × 9,200 = 18,400 input tok/s, Ch17 §17.4.2); the summed ~19K 
figure must not be compared to a prefill-only ceiling (Ch17) — but that is the prefill-only ideal, not 
the goodput: the benchmark’s goodput is the SLO-windowed prefill+decode figure under continuous 
batching, and it is the benchmark (not the ceiling) that confirms the SLO is met at that concurrency. 
Candidate B (a smaller 7B) shows 0.6 s TTFT — much faster — but scores lower on the capability 
screen for the retrieval-QA quality threshold. We therefore select A for deployment (capability + 
deployment both pass), not B (capability fails despite speed). The benchmark did not score one 
number; it reproduced the decision. [ILLUSTRATIVE][DERIVED]

172
14.4. Measurement
Four habits make benchmarks trustworthy:
1. Use the model’s own tokenizer on representative real prompts. The “4 chars ≈ 1 token” 
estimate can be 2-5× off for code/JSON/technical text (Ch6).
2. Measure distributions, not means — log p50/p90/p95/p99 for every latency metric and the 
full token-length distribution. A 32K-tail context is a different workload than a 9.2K average.
3. Reproduce the concurrency and burst — a benchmark must hit the model at our peak 
concurrency, not a single request, or it won’t show the queuing and KV contention that break 
SLOs.
4. Include goodput and cost, not just latency — the SLO-windowed throughput and $/request 
complete the picture for the economics (Ch16).
14.5. Common Mistakes
• Citing a capability leaderboard as proof of serving fitness. MMLU does not predict TTFT; 
it screens capability only.
• Benchmarking a single request. Real SLOs break under concurrency; a single-request 
benchmark hides the tail.
• Mean-only latency reporting. A 0.84 s mean hides a 5 s tail that breaches the p95 SLO (Ch6).
• Using the wrong tokenizer estimate. Under-counting context via the 4-char heuristic 
mis-sizes KV cache and prefill.
• Optimizing raw throughput past the SLO window. Bigger batch sizes raise tokens/s but 
push TTFT over the deadline, zeroing goodput (Ch6, Ch11).
14.6. Architecture Consequence
The benchmark is the bridge from candidate architectures (Ch12) to selection and then to TCO 
(Ch16):
• Capability screen narrows the candidate models (from Ch3/Ch5 model-selection) to those 
that meet the accuracy/quality threshold.
• Deployment benchmark then measures each surviving candidate under the canonical 
workload, producing the TTFT/goodput/cost numbers that drive the choice.
• The chosen system becomes the basis for the TCO and performance-engineering work of 
Ch15-16.
The benchmark is not a one-time event; it is the recurring production validation that catches 
drift (model updates, traffic shape changes, memory pressure) before they breach the SLO.

14.7. What We Still Don’t Know
• How closely capability benchmarks predict enterprise Q&A quality for a given domain 
is [UNRESOLVED]; a custom retrieval-QA screen on real documents is stronger but costly.
• Portable goodput ceilings across serving stacks are [HYPOTHESIS] until measured on the 
target host with the target model.
• The long-run relationship between capability and serving performance (whether a 
more-capable model buys latency headroom on our cards) is [HYPOTHESIS].
14.8. End-of-Chapter Mini-Case: Capability Screen vs. Deployment Benchmark
A team of skeptical engineers tells the architect: “The new model tops MMLU, we should just deploy 
it.” Applying this chapter, the architect separates the claims. Yes, the model passes the capability 
screen for retrieval-QA quality. But the architect refuses to stop there: they build a deployment 
benchmark reproducing the canonical workload — real prompts at ~9.2K in / ~300 out, 40 rps peak 
(≈344 in-flight, Ch17), the SLO (TTFT median ≤ 1.2 s, p95 ≤ 2 s; TPOT ~25 ms) — and measure the 
new model on the existing hardware. The result: the leaderboard-topping model’s tokenizer produces 
more tokens for the representative prompt corpus (a larger token count for the same text) and it 
has slower prefill, driving p95 TTFT to 2.6 s — breaching the SLO that the incumbent 70B met at 
1.9 s. The team does not deploy the leaderboard winner. The architect’s discipline — screen with 
capability, select with deployment — prevented a service degradation that a single MMLU score 
would have caused. The benchmark, done right, did not rank models; it predicted experience and 
saved the service. 

chapter 15.
Performance Engineering: From “It Works” to 
“It Meets the SLO”
The Architect’s Question
We are tasked with engineering performance for a serving layer that must absorb the day-to-day 
traffic of a growing user base while delivering acceptable latency. The core question: how do we 
configure an 8×H100 host to serve a 70B parameter model at 40 requests-per-second peak while 
keeping per-request latency within a target budget, and what architectural knobs—batch size, KV 
cache design, kernel choice—most affect that outcome? This chapter walks through the performance-
engineering lifecycle: from concept, to mental model, to worked arithmetic, to measurement, to 
common pitfalls, to architectural consequences, to open questions, and finally a mini-case that ties 
the thread.
15.1. Concept
Performance engineering in LLM serving is the discipline of matching compute, memory, and I/O 
throughput to the stochastic workload of token generation and prompt processing. Unlike traditional 
web serving, where request‑cycle overhead dominates the tail, the dominant cost in LLM inference is 
the arithmetic intensity of transformer matrix multiplications and the memory bandwidth consumed 
by KV cache reads and writes on every token step. The serving system must therefore optimize 
three inter‑dependent quantities: token throughput (tokens/s), per-token latency, and GPU memory 
utilization. These are governed by the model FLOP count, the chosen kernel (e.g. FlashAttention 
vs. scaled‑dot‑product), and the batching strategy (static vs. dynamic, continuous vs. chunked). The 
architect’s first move is to identify which resource—compute, bandwidth, or latency—is the primary 
175

Performance Engineering: From “It Works” to “It Meets the SLO”
176
constraint for the canonical scenario, then apply the appropriate optimization to shift the bottleneck. 
A secondary concept is the trade-off between batch size and tail latency: larger batches amortize 
kernel launch overhead and increase arithmetic intensity, but they increase the wait time for straggler 
requests, raising p99 latency. The designer must therefore balance arithmetic gain against tail‑latency 
penalty, a tension that shapes every later architectural decision.
15.2. Mental Model
We model the serving request lifecycle as a pipeline of computational phases with a cross‑cutting 
state‑management plane. The compute phases run in order: (1) request preprocessing / tokenization, 
(2) prefill, (3) iterative model decode, (4) token sampling / selection, (5) detokenization / streaming. 
KV management is not a temporal stage after generation — allocation, growth, paging, reuse, 
eviction, and (in a disaggregated deployment) transfer occur throughout the whole request lifetime, 
so we draw it as a state‑management plane that spans prefill and decode rather than a step that 
follows them. Each compute phase has a resource ceiling: compute‑bound phases are limited by 
TFLOPS, memory‑bound phases by GB/s of GPU HBM bandwidth. The architecture designer must 
identify which phase is bottlenecked for the canonical scenario and then apply the appropriate 
optimization—kernel fusion, cache reuse, or batching—to shift the bottleneck. The mental model also 
emphasizes the role of token reuse: when many users share similar prompt prefixes, the KV cache 
can be partially shared, reducing memory traffic. This reuse effect is difficult to quantify without 
workload profiling, but it is a first‑order consideration in multi‑tenant serving. The model also 
captures the interaction between sequence length and memory cost: attention quadraticities (O(L²) 
FLOPs and O(L²) memory per head once the QKT /softmax score matrix is materialized) mean that 
long contexts (9,000+ tokens) disproportionately strain both compute and HBM bandwidth, making 
kernel choice (FlashAttention vs. SDPA) one of the first kernel‑level optimizations to validate for 
long‑context prefill — not a demonstrated global ranking, since prefix reuse, KV precision, batching 
policy, P/D scheduling, weight precision, admission control, and simply provisioning more prefill 
capacity can each dominate depending on the measured workload.
15.3. Worked Example
Canonical scenario: ~2,000 registered users, ~5% concurrent (100 users), ~10 rps average / ~40 rps 
peak, prompt ~9,200 input tokens + ~300 output, the canonical 80-layer, hidden-8192, full-MHA 70B 
reference model (a 70B-class dense FP16 model), 8×H100 host with 640 GB GPU memory.
Derived quantity 1 – concurrent users: 2,000 ×0.05 = 100 users.
Derived quantity 2 – peak token throughput demand: At 40 rps the workload mixes prefill 
(input) and decode (output) tokens, and they hit the hardware differently. Prefill demand = 40 rps × 
9,200 = 368,000 input tokens/s; decode demand = 40 rps × 300 = 12,000 output tokens/s. These two 
regimes must not be collapsed into one “380,000 tokens/s” when reasoning about compute, because 

177
Performance Engineering: From “It Works” to “It Meets the SLO”
long-prompt prefill commonly occupies a higher-arithmetic-intensity regime and low-batch decode 
commonly occupies a lower-arithmetic-intensity regime; whether either phase is actually compute- 
or bandwidth-bound is a property of the model × workload × kernel × hardware operating point 
(Chapter 8).
A hard terminology distinction the rest of this book relies on. Do not conflate two separate 
statements:
• Kernel regime — the arithmetic intensity of the kernel relative to the roofline ridge (is this 
one kernel compute-bound or bandwidth-bound?).
• Workload capacity deficit — the aggregate offered demand relative to the achievable service 
capacity (does the deployment have enough compute/bandwidth/concurrency to absorb this 
arrival rate?).
A workload can exceed a deployment’s capacity even when the underlying kernel is not compute-
bound; conversely, a compute-bound kernel does not imply the deployed fleet lacks compute. In 
the canonical scenario at peak, prefill exerts a large aggregate compute demand (a capacity-deficit 
signal), while the prefill kernel’s high arithmetic intensity is what makes it land on the compute 
plateau of the roofline — two different facts that happen to co-occur. Keep them separate.
Compute demand versus peak — and why this is not an MFU: A 70B FP16 transformer needs ≈2 
×70×10⁹ FLOPs per generated token. The instantaneous prefill compute demand is 368,000 ×2 × 70×10⁹ 
≈ 5.15 ×10¹⁶ FLOPs/s, against a theoretical FP16-dense peak of 8 ×989 TFLOPS = 7.91 ×10¹⁵ FLOPs/s 
for the 8×H100 host (989 TFLOPS per GPU, Chapter 8 canonical). Taken naively this ratio is ~6.5× — 
a demand that exceeds a single host’s peak. That is a legitimate and important architectural signal: a 
single 8×H100 host cannot prefill the peak arrival rate on the naive dense-FLOP model, so prefill 
either needs to be spread across burst headroom, handled by prompt/prefix caching, disaggregated 
(P/D separation), or absorbed by queueing headroom at off-peak. What it must not be called is 
“MFU = 6.5”: MFU (Model FLOPs Utilization) is by definition achieved realities divided by peak — 
a utilization ratio that cannot exceed 1.0. Presenting a ratio above 1 for a utilization metric is a 
unit/modeling error. The honest formulation separates the two quantities. The first is a congestion 
check: is the arrival-rate FLOP demand above or below peak? Here it is ~6.5× above for prefill, so 
the host is prefill-starved at peak and must lean on caching/batching/queueing. The second is an 
achieved MFU: measured device FLOPs ÷ peak, which we only report from a real benchmark. For 
illustration we suppose it lands in the 30–40 % range for FlashAttention-based inference. The decode 
figure at 12,000 tokens/s is ≈ 1.68 ×10¹⁵ ÷ 7.91 ×10¹⁵ ≈ 0.21, i.e. ~21 % — again a groundable number, 
not a ceiling-defying one; the actual achieved value must come from the target serving benchmark. 
The architectural conclusion, that there is no single bottleneck for the whole request, stands — and it 
must be argued phase by phase. At the canonical peak, prefill shows a workload capacity deficit — 
aggregate compute demand above the host’s peak (it needs more FLOPs/s than the host can supply, 
and leans on caching/queueing). Decode is primarily memory-bandwidth constrained, and maximum 
concurrency is constrained by KV residency. Each phase is established from arithmetic intensity and 

Performance Engineering: From “It Works” to “It Meets the SLO”
178
the roofline (Chapter 8), never from an impossible “MFU > 1.” None of them is the same statement as 
“the prefill kernel is compute-bound.” The ~6.5× figure is a lower-bound impossibility check (the 
host cannot meet peak on the naive dense model), not a fleet-sizing estimate: the real fleet count 
comes from the achieved prefill tokens/s under the actual latency SLO and representative traffic 
mix (Ch 17/20), and it is sensitive to cache-hit rate, input-length distribution, batch schedule, and 
sustainable efficiency — the 6.5× only says “more than one host is required,” not “how many.”
KV cache memory footprint: The KV cache grows linearly with cached context and layers; it 
must never be computed per-layer and then treated as the whole-model total. For the canonical 
full-MHA reference geometry (80 layers, hidden dimension 8,192 — the 70B-class teaching model, not 
a universal 70B property), the per-token footprint across ALL layers is 2 × layers × hidden × bytes = 
2 ×80 ×8,192 ×2 = 2,621,440 B ≈ 2.62 MB/token (2.5 MiB; canonical value from Chapter 7) — not the 
32 KB that a single-layer calculation would suggest. For a 9,500-token context, one user’s KV cache is 
therefore ≈9,500 ×2.62 MB ≈ 24.9 GB, not ~304 MB. For the canonical workload’s ~86 average in-flight 
requests at 10 rps (L_exec = λ × W_exec ≈ 10 × 8.6; see Ch 4 — note ~86 is the request occupancy, 
distinct from the ~100 active-user population), the total KV cache would be ≈86 × 24.9 GB ≈ 2,140 GB 
— which does not fit the 640 GB GPU pool; using the canonical practical KV budget (640 GB − 140 GB 
weights − ~64 GB runtime/workspace ≈ 436 GB), only ≈17.5 concurrent requests (9,500-token KV each) 
fit on-host for KV (436 GB ÷ 24.9037 GB; conservative integer floor 17 (aggregate bound; per-rank 
and fragmentation must be checked separately)), before also reserving weight residency. This is 
a decisive, order-of-magnitude correction to the intuitive picture: long-context KV is a first-order 
memory constraint, and the architect sizes concurrency by KV capacity, not by the small per-layer 
number. Doubling context to 19,000 tokens pushes per-user KV to ~49.8 GB (19,000 × 2.62 MB) and 
halves on-host concurrency to ~8.7 (436 GB ÷ 49.8 GB; conservative integer floor 8). The canonical 
unit-check rule (quantity/unit/formula/substitution/result/sanity) catches this class of error: if 9,500 
tokens × ~2.5 MB/token ≈ 24 GB does not reconcile with the intended ~304 MB, the unit layer-count 
has been dropped.
Attention FLOP comparison: Scaled‑dot‑product attention (SDPA) for 9,200‑token prompts. Note 
the 2P/token figure above approximates the parameterized dense‑matmul work per token (2 × pa­
rameters); it does not capture all sequence‑length‑dependent attention work, which is accounted 
for separately here. Attention is two matmuls per token per head — Q·KT then ·V — each a mul­
tiply‑accumulate of L²·d, so in FLOPs (2 per MAC) the per‑layer cost is ≈ 4 × L² × d_model, where 
L=9,200 and d_model=8,192. That is 4 × 9,200² × 8,192 ≈ 2.8 × 10¹² FLOPs per layer (the full 64 heads; 
the per‑head figure 4 × 9,200² × 128 ≈ 4.3 × 10¹⁰ must be multiplied by the number of heads, not 
quoted alone). Across 80 layers that is ≈ 2.2 × 10¹⁴ FLOPs — the quadratic attention term that grows 
dominant at long context, matching Chapter 8’s 4·n_layers·L²·d formula: the attention term is ≈17% 
of the parameter-matmul FLOPs (≈15% of total forward FLOPs) at 9.2K (Fig 22.1).
FlashAttention is not “faster attention” in the sense that it does less arithmetic. It computes the 
exact same Q·KT , softmax, ·V sequence. What changes is where the intermediate lives: a naive kernel

I/O bound: FlashAttention — Tri Dao et al., arXiv:2205.14135.
figure 15.1: FlashAttention: the same math, far less HBM traffic. Before (naive / SDPA), the interme­
diate attention matrix is materialized to HBM and re-read, so every query block re-streams the full 
K/V row-block — many HBM round-trips. After (FlashAttention), the computation is tiled and fused 
so each K/V tile is read into on-chip memory once for that tile and the result is accumulated in place — 
far fewer HBM round-trips. Scope of the “read once” claim: the tile-level on-chip reuse is per-tile; 
it is not a guarantee that every K/V element is read exactly once globally across all query blocks (the 
exact number of K/V re-reads depends on the tile scheduling, sequence length, and SRAM size — see 
the original I/O analysis in the FlashAttention papers). The attention result is the same mathematical 
target; the gain is fewer HBM round-trips, not a change to the attention mathematics. (Floating-point 
caveat: the tiled accumulation order differs from the naive path, so results are equivalent in exact 
arithmetic but can differ in the last bits under finite precision — a numerical-equivalence, not a 
bit-identical, claim. I/O bound: Tri Dao et al., “FlashAttention,” NeurIPS 2022, arXiv:2205.14135.)
Read the figure as an I/O story, not a speed-up number.

Performance Engineering: From “It Works” to “It Meets the SLO”
180
writes the L×L attention matrix to HBM and reads it back, so the materialized intermediate itself 
is O(L²) in memory; FlashAttention avoids materializing that full L×L matrix in HBM and instead 
tiles the computation so each tile stays in on-chip registers/shared memory with the softmax done 
online — substantially reducing HBM reads/writes through tiled on-chip computation (the precise I/O 
complexity is more nuanced than a clean quadratic-to-linear swap, but the materialized-intermediate 
traffic is what drops). Because the arithmetic intensity — FLOP/byte — rises (same FLOPs, far fewer 
bytes moved), the kernel moves up the hardware ladder of Fig 8.1 toward the compute-bound region, 
even though the model and the math are unchanged. That is the whole reason the speed-up is 
workload-dependent: it is largest for long contexts where the O(L²) intermediate dominates, and 
negligible for short ones where HBM traffic is already small. It is also why the improvement should 
be measured (Ch. 14) rather than quoted from a published “2–3×” figure that was never portable.
15.4. Measurement
To validate the mental model we instrument the serving stack with three metric families: (a) 
token latency percentiles (p50, p95, p99) per request type, (b) GPU utilization (SM active, memory 
bandwidth via Nsight), and (c) KV cache size over time. A minimal benchmark runs 40 rps with 
warm‑up, records the p95 token latency, and measures the GPU memory fraction consumed by KV 
cache. Repeating with different batch sizes (1, 4, 16, 32) reveals the throughput‑latency knee. The 
illustrative numbers below are scenario values to be measured on the target stack, not vendor or 
published figures: beyond batch‑4 the p95 latency typically rises (implying a knee near batch 4) 
because kernel launch overhead is amortized but inter‑request token interleaving increases cache 
thrashing. On the FlashAttention “traffic reduction” claim, be precise about the units: one 
often sees a FlashAttention‑class kernel reported at a different achieved HBM bandwidth than a naive 
SDPA kernel (illustratively ~150 GB/s vs ~400 GB/s for 9,500‑token contexts). That is a rate, not a 
byte count. A bandwidth figure alone does not establish that fewer bytes/request were transferred 
— it only shows the kernel’s achieved memory throughput. To claim a traffic reduction, compare 
the bytes transferred per request (or read/write transaction counters) under a matched workload; 
the FlashAttention advantage shows up there as fewer materialized/intermediate bytes, not as a 
low raw bandwidth. And separate the two cache concepts: “cold‑start KV warm‑up” (populating 
the KV-cache blocks for a new context) is different from “model‑weight loading” (streaming the 
140 GB weights into HBM) and from CUDA/JIT kernel warm‑up. A 70B cold‑start that reuses cached 
weights is far faster than one that must load them from scratch — but these are distinct effects, not 
one number. Set these illustrative figures from the target host’s profiler before quoting them as fact.

the prefill work per repeated prompt (left plot) so more prefill‑recompute headroom remains under 
the same host. They are not competing on the same axis.
15.5. The Optimization Decision Map
The measurement section tells us what is slow; this map tells us which lever to try next. It is organized 
as a symptom → bottleneck → evidence → candidate mechanism chain, so the architect diagnoses 
before acting rather than applying a known technique by fashion. Every mechanism below is a 
hypothesis to benchmark, not a guaranteed win — each trades one resource for another, and 
whether it helps depends on the measured workload and hardware (Ch. 14).

Two cautions. First, do not read the table as “technique X fixes symptom Y”: the same symptom 
can come from two bottlenecks (high TPOT with a bandwidth-saturated GPU is different from high 
TPOT with a capacity-capped GPU), so the evidence column matters as much as the symptom. Second, 
the last two rows (P/D disaggregation, interconnect tuning) are the expensive, structural levers — 
they are worth testing only after the cheaper in-kernel and in-scheduler mechanisms have been 
measured and shown insufficient, because they change capacity, orchestration, and cost, not just a 
kernel constant.
15.6. Two Mechanisms, Read as Mechanisms
Two entries in the map deserve their mechanism spelled out, because they are frequently cited by 
reported speed-up numbers rather than by how they work.

Performance Engineering: From “It Works” to “It Meets the SLO”
184
Speculative decoding. The idea is to turn one slow serial step into a draft-then-verify pair. A 
small, cheap draft model proposes a candidate sequence of tokens; the larger target model then 
verifies all of them in a single batched forward pass, accepting the ones that match its own distribution. 
The loop is: draft → propose candidates → target verifies → accept a prefix → continue from the last 
accepted token. Why this can be faster: the expensive part of decode is not the arithmetic per token 
but the serialization — each target-model step streams the weights from HBM. If the draft can propose 
k tokens that the target accepts in one verification pass, the target’s expensive weight-stream is spent 
once on k tokens instead of k times on one — a genuine reduction in HBM round-trips (the same 
resource the FlashAttention discussion is about, but at the whole-model scale). Why it is not free: the 
draft costs extra compute, the verification pass itself has cost, and the acceptance rate (how many 
of the proposed tokens the target actually accepts) decides the win — a low acceptance rate means 
the draft and verification are spent for almost nothing. It is therefore workload/model/hardware 
dependent: a draft that closely matches the target (or a workload that is highly predictable) accepts 
more. No published speed-up is portable: a “6×” from one model/paper is a statement about 
that evaluation, not a guarantee for a different model or serving stack — measure it on the target 
workload or treat it as a hypothesis.
One subtlety about “matching its own distribution”: the target does not simply accept the draft 
tokens that happen to agree. Under stochastic sampling, accepting a proposed token outright (rather 
than by the exact-speculative-sampling correction that re-weighs each accepted and rejected token by 
the target’s posterior) can bias the output distribution away from the target model’s true distribution. 
The distribution-preserving result holds only when each proposed token is accepted or rejected by the 
exact acceptance/sampling procedure (the modified-rejection-sampling step of Leviathan et al., “Fast 
Inference from Transformers via Speculative Decoding,” arXiv:2211.17192; see the Sources chapter), 
which re-normalizes the continuation at each step. For greedy/argmax decoding the agreement test 
is exact; for temperature/prompt-driven sampling it is not, and the acceptance criterion must be the 
correction procedure, not mere token equality.
Chunked prefill. A very long prompt produces a large prefill operation that can monopolize the 
compute for hundreds of milliseconds, stalling concurrent latency-sensitive decode work. Chunked 
prefill splits that prefill into smaller pieces and interleaves them with decode steps in the scheduler, 
so the GPU keeps serving decode between prefill chunks instead of blocking on one burst. The causal 
chain: long prompt → large prefill → scheduler/resource monopolization → decode interference 
→ TTFT and TPOT both degrade → chunked prefill as a scheduling mechanism to bound the 
per-phase burst. The trade-off is not “smaller is better”: a smaller chunk reduces the decode stall 
but adds scheduler overhead (more per-chunk bookkeeping, potentially lower prefill throughput 
from less parallelism within a chunk), so chunk size is an empirically-chosen middle — measure the 
goodput/latency knee rather than assume.

185
Performance Engineering: From “It Works” to “It Meets the SLO”
15.7. Common Mistakes
Over‑provisioning batch size to chase throughput: Increasing batch size improves TFLOPS utiliza­
tion but degrades p99 latency because tail requests wait for larger chunks to fill. The designer must 
balance batch‑size‑driven arithmetic gain against tail‑latency penalty. A practical illustrative rule of 
thumb: choose the smallest batch size that keeps MFU above ~25 % while keeping p95 latency under 
the SLO target (the 25 % threshold and the knee are scenario values — measure them on the target 
workload). Ignoring KV cache growth: A 70B model with 9,500‑token contexts consumes ≈24.9 GB of 
KV per user (≈2.62 MB/token × 9,500, max end-of-generation). Without cache eviction, compression, 
or a concurrency cap, even ~17.5 concurrent requests would exhaust the host’s canonical practical 
KV budget (~436 GB after weights + runtime), so 100 concurrent full-context users require either 
multiple hosts, much shorter contexts, or KV compression (FP8/8-bit). But be precise about what 
“evicting” a context means, because it is not one action. Three distinct mechanisms exist. (a) 
Evicting an idle/inactive prefix-cache entry — a cached prefix not currently in service — is safe, because 
no active request depends on it and it can be recomputed on reuse. (b) Offloading or recomputing 
the KV of an active request whose context is still being generated changes nothing about semantics, 
but it costs a recompute or a transfer when the request resumes. (c) Deliberate sliding-window or 
context-truncation strategies drop older tokens of an active request’s context. This changes the 
model’s effective context: it alters what the attention can attend to, and it is a semantics change, 
not merely a memory optimization. LRU of inactive cached prefixes is different from truncating 
active KV. Do not conflate them. KV quantization is a separate, orthogonal lever that cuts the 
per-token footprint without changing semantics (FP8 ≈2.62 → ~1.31 MB/token — exactly 50% of BF16 
by raw tensor byte-halving, the same FP8 constant defined in Ch 7; vLLM’s cited 54% [S6] is a decode 
inter-token-latency slope, a latency metric, not a memory ratio, and any allocator block/metadata 
overhead is not vendor-measured). Using scaled‑dot‑product attention without FlashAttention: For 
long contexts (9,200+ tokens) the quadratic memory cost of SDPA (O(L²)) dominates the FLOP cost. 
FlashAttention fuses read‑write‑compute and reduces HBM traffic by ~3–5× ([ILLUSTRATIVE] — 
kernel- and length-dependent; measure bytes/request on the target stack), raising effective arith­
metic intensity. This is one of the highest-impact kernel-level choices (it sits among prefix caching, 
KV-precision, batching, and P/D disaggregation as a major lever, not the single deciding one), and 
it pays off specifically when memory-bound; for moderate lengths (2,000–4,000 tokens) the HBM 
bandwidth reduction typically translates to a ~1.3–1.8× throughput gain on H100 (illustrative — bench 
the specific kernel on the target hardware). Neglecting kernel launch overhead: In dynamic‑batching 
setups, each new token generation can trigger a kernel launch. Without continuous batching or 
token‑level fusion, the kernel-launch overhead (illustratively ~10–20 % of GPU time on H100-class 
parts at this traffic) can artificially depress measured MFU.

Performance Engineering: From “It Works” to “It Meets the SLO”
186
15.8. Architecture Consequence
The measurement and mistake analysis drive three architecture decisions for the canonical 
deployment: (1) FlashAttention‑2 as the default attention kernel, fused with RoPE and QKV projection 
to minimize HBM transactions; (2) continuous batching, with the maximum chunk size chosen 
empirically from the measured goodput/latency knee (a chunk size of 8 tokens is an [ILLUSTRATIVE] 
configuration, not the recommendation — the knee must be measured on the target stack); (3) KV 
cache management via FP8 KV quantization (≈2.62 → ~1.31 MB/token — exactly 50% of BF16 by raw 
tensor byte-halving, cutting a 9,500‑token per‑user KV to ~12.45 GB; vLLM’s 54% [S6] is a decode 
inter-token-latency slope, a latency metric, not a memory ratio, and any block/metadata overhead 
is not vendor-measured) plus length‑based eviction, so that under the same ~436 GB practical KV 
budget on‑host concurrency rises from ~17.5 to ~35.0 full‑context users (436 GB ÷ 12.45 GB). These 
choices together are expected to raise achieved token throughput from ~8,000 tokens/s (raw, no 
flash, batch‑1) toward an illustrative ~35,000–70,000 tokens/s [ILLUSTRATIVE achievable range] 
(with flash, continuous batch) — the exact figure must come from the target benchmark — while 
keeping the host’s 640 GB for weights plus KV. A fourth decision, borne out of the mini‑case (Ch11 
§11.8), is to design the request‑routing layer for KV cache affinity: when a user re‑submits a request, 
routing to the same GPU avoids cache warm‑up penalties and keeps p99 latency under the target 
SLO.
15.9. What We Still Don’t Know
How does token‑level sparsity in user prompts affect the actual FLOP/token cost, and can we exploit 
it without accuracy loss? Sparse prompt patterns (e.g. few‑shot prefixes, cached query prefixes) could 
reduce the effective FLOP count, but the savings depend on the sparsity pattern and the attention 
implementation. What is the optimal continuous‑batch chunk size as a function of request arrival 
distribution (Poisson vs. bursty) and tail‑latency targets? Analytical models assume Poisson arrivals, but 
real user traffic exhibits bursts (e.g. social‑media events) that may require larger chunks or alternative 
back‑pressure mechanisms. Can in‑flight KV cache compression (e.g. speculative decoding–guided 
pruning) reduce memory pressure without increasing p99 latency? Pruning less‑important key/value 
pairs based on attention weight magnitudes is promising, but the accuracy‑latency trade‑off needs 
empirical validation across model families. How does multi‑tenant isolation—guarding one user’s cache 
from another’s—translate to multi‑GPU scaling when the model exceeds a single GPU’s 80 GB? Model 
parallelism (pipeline or tensor) adds communication overhead; the performance cost of moving KV 
cache between GPUs during tenant rotation is an open question.
15.10. End-of-Chapter Mini-Case: The Second Host: a Kernel/Batching Gain, Not 
a Capacity Win
A new deployment adds 500 registered users (traffic rises to ~12.5/50 rps at the same 5 % concur­
rency profile). The team provisions a second 8×H100 host and (in this worked example) runs the 

187
Performance Engineering: From “It Works” to “It Meets the SLO”
benchmark with continuous batching and FlashAttention‑2. The p95 queueing/scheduling delay 
ahead of prefill — not TTFT, which at this profile is dominated by the canonical ~1.08 s prefill 
(Chapters 4/8; Chapter 14 measures p95 TTFT at 1.9 s for this stack) — drops from 320 ms (single 
host, batch‑1) to 140 ms (dual host, continuous batch), and token throughput rises from 8,000 to 
70,000 tokens/s. The MFU stabilizes at 38 %. These are [ILLUSTRATIVE SCENARIO RESULT] values 
— an illustrative benchmark outcome, not a home-lab measurement; the exact figures must come 
from a deployment benchmark on the target hardware/stack (Chapter 14). The remaining question 
is whether the cross‑host request routing layer can maintain affinity KV cache residency, avoiding 
cache warm‑up penalties on re‑requests. The ∼20 % affinity throughput gain and the ∼2.1 s cold-start 
warm-up cost are themselves [ILLUSTRATIVE SCENARIO RESULT] values, not measured results.
Do not read the 8.75× throughput jump as a capacity-side win. It is dominated by the kernel/batch­
ing change (batch‑1 no‑flash → continuous batching with FlashAttention‑2), which is worth most of 
the multiplicative gain, plus ~2× from adding the second host — the two are not separable from 
this single outcome number. This is a per‑host kernel/batching improvement story, not a claim that 
“more GPUs = 8.75×.” Were we adding a second host with no kernel/batching change, the expected 
throughput gain would be roughly the host-pool ratio (≈2×), and the fleet-sizing consequence is 
computed separately in Ch 16–17 from the service-time model, not from this mini-case’s aggregate 
number.

chapter 16.
TCO: The Total Cost Reality Check
The Architect’s Question
After this chapter we should be able to answer the question every stakeholder actually asks — what 
does this really cost? — with a defensible number, not a guess. We will build a total-cost-of-ownership 
model that separates capital, operating, and opportunity costs, then price the canonical workload 
across three delivery modes: self-hosted on our own cards, cloud GPU instances, and a managed 
inference API. After this chapter, “we save money by self-hosting” is either proven or refuted by 
arithmetic, and the break-even point between modes is explicit.
16.1. Concept
Total Cost of Ownership (TCO) is the sum of everything required to deliver a capability over its 
lifetime, not just the price tag of the hardware or the per-token rate. It has three cost families:
1. Capital (capex) — the one-time cost of the cards, servers, networking, racks, and cooling that 
make up the system. For self-hosted inference this dominates up front: an 8×H100 server with 
networking and infrastructure carries a large price. [ILLUSTRATIVE][DERIVED]
2. Operating (opex) — the recurring cost of running it: electricity and cooling, staff, soft­
ware/licenses, cloud instance rental, and replacement/amortization of hardware. For cloud and 
managed API this is the whole cost; for self-hosted it compounds on capex.
3. Opportunity — the cost of the alternative not taken: the team time spent operating vs building, 
the delay to market, and the engineering hours that do not scale with either card or token 
counts. This is the most omitted and often the largest term.
189

TCO: The Total Cost Reality Check
190
The architect’s job is not to minimize capex, nor opex, nor provider bill — it is to minimize the 
number that matters for the organization’s decision, which usually means total cost per useful 
request at the required quality and SLO.
16.2. Mental Model
TCO is a utilization-weighted average over the whole system, not a sticker price. Three 
mental moves keep the arithmetic honest:
• Amortize, don’t stare at the price tag. A $400K server is an asset consumed over ~5 years; 
spread it into dollars-per-month, then into dollars-per-request, before comparing to a per-token 
API rate. [ILLUSTRATIVE][DERIVED]
• Follow the token, not the GPU. The thing being bought is completed, SLO-meeting requests. 
Two systems with the same card count can differ 10× in cost per request if one has 3× the 
goodput per card, better utilization, or no idle coasting.
• Include what does not scale. Staff, power, networking engineering, and the latency of 
procurement are sunk or recurring whether utilization is 20% or 80%. A low-cost-per-card 
system that burns engineers can be the most expensive.
The decision shape is a break-even graph: at low volume, managed/cloud-and-shut-down wins 
(no idle capex); at high volume, self-hosted cards amortize below the per-token rate. The crossover 
point is what we compute.
16.3. Worked Example
16.3.1. Size the fleet first, then price it
TCO is meaningless until we know how many hosts the workload actually requires. A common 
sizing mistake is to start from a single host: a single 8×H100 host can only sustain ~2.0 req/s under 
the KV/latency analytical planning bound (Chapter 17/20), so it cannot serve even the canonical 10 
rps average, let alone the 40 rps peak. We therefore size the fleet first (Ch 17) and price it second.
Step 1 — fleet size. The canonical workload requires N_hosts = max(N_throughput, N_KV, N_
SLO). With the ~8.6 s service time and C≈17.5 KV-resident requests/host (Ch 17; byte-accurate 17.51, 
conservative integer floor 17):
Sizing bound

So the canonical workload’s peak requires roughly 20 hosts (FP16 KV, full utilization; 29 working 
hosts at the 70% utilization target — 30 total with N−1), and even the average needs ~5 — a fleet, not 
one box. (FP8 KV roughly halves this to ~10 hosts at peak — C ≈ 35.0, so ⌈344/35.0⌉ = 10, matching 
Ch 17.) Every TCO figure below is scaled to this fleet requirement; pricing a single host 
would understate the cost by roughly 5–20×.
What kind of number is ~2.0 req/s? The per-host rate C_KV / W ≈ 17.5 / 8.6 ≈ 2.0 
req/s is an upper-order planning estimate under a particular concurrency, timing, and cache geometry 
— not an observed service rate. It is an average-in-system relation, not a measured goodput. The three 
quantities behind N_hosts = max(N_throughput, N_KV, N_SLO) are three different models 
that must be derived and measured separately, and the formula is only as strong as the weakest 
one:
• N_KV (KV-memory occupancy ceiling) — how many requests reside in KV cache given the 
host’s usable HBM and the per-token KV footprint. This is an arithmetic allocation bound (the 
~17.5 figure).
• N_throughput (phase capacity) — how many requests the host can complete per second 
given the service time. The ~8.6 s service time is an uncontended request-level estimate, and 
C/W treats it as fixed.
• N_SLO (queueing / goodput) — the number of hosts needed to hold p95/p99 TTFT, TPOT, 
and success rate under concurrent arrival. Little’s law relates average in-system work to 
elapsed time under stability; it does not independently prove a throughput ceiling or 
a p99 latency. At concurrency ~17, TTFT and TPOT can differ substantially from the isolated 
~8.6 s used to obtain C/W, because batch efficiency, per-request duration, and interference are 
all coupled to load.
So the 20-host figure is a scenario-specific bound contingent on the 8.6-second service 
time remaining valid under concurrent execution, not a deployment-validated estimate. Before 
budgeting against it, measure λ_goodput per host at the target p95/p99 TTFT, TPOT, and success 
rate on the actual workload, and re-derive N_hosts. (See the falsification protocol in Ch 17.)
Treat both provisioning policies explicitly. The headline $ figures below use the full-
utilization fleet (≈20 hosts, provisioned exactly at the 40 rps peak with no headroom). The capacity-
planning rule (Ch 17 Table 17-1) uses a 70% max-operating-utilization target, which gives 29 working 
hosts at the peak ⌈40×8.6/(17.5×0.7)⌉ = ⌈344/12.25⌉ = 29; because one host must be able to drain 
(failover/maintenance) while the fleet still clears the peak, the N−1 policy requires 30 hosts total

(29 working + 1 spare — 29 surviving × 2.0 × 0.70 ≈ 40.6 ≥ 40 rps). That spare is a reserve host, not 
serving capacity. Both outcomes belong in the decision:

Provisioning sensitivity (explicit): the TCO figures below assume the full-utilization fleet (≈20 
hosts, provisioned exactly at the 40 rps peak without headroom) so that the cost comparison 
is not double-counting idle capacity. If instead the fleet is provisioned at the 70% utilization target 
that the sizing rule (Ch17 Table 17-1) actually uses — 30 hosts (29 working + 1 N−1 spare) — 
capex scales to ≈30/20 = 1.5×: ≈$174K capex (30 × ~$5.8K/host/mo) + ≈$33K opex + ≈$10K staff 
≈ $217K/month, and per-request cost rises to ≈ $217K / 26M ≈ $8.4 per 1,000 requests (vs the $5.7/1K below). 
The durable conclusion — self-host loses to scale-to-load cloud on this workload — holds under either 
provisioning policy; the specific $5.7/1K number is the full-utilization figure and should be read with 
that assumption.
Step 2 — price each mode on N_hosts ≈ 20 (FP16, provisioned for the 40 rps peak).
Mode 1 — self-hosted on owned cards (20 × 8×H100).
• Capex: ~$350K per fully built 8×H100 server, amortized over 5 years → ~$5.8K/month/host → 
~$116K/month for ~20 hosts. [ILLUSTRATIVE][DERIVED], scenario value [ILLUSTRATIVE]
• Opex (power + staff): per host, 8 H100 GPUs at ~700 W ≈ 5.6 kW GPU IT + ~2.2 kW host/net­
work ≈ 7.8 kW IT, × PUE ~1.3 ≈ ~10.1 kW at the wall; at ~$0.15/kWh always-on that is 
~$1.1K/month/host → ~$22K/month for ~20 hosts. Staff/engineering alloc ~$10K/month. 
[ILLUSTRATIVE][DERIVED], scenario value [ILLUSTRATIVE]
• Monthly total ≈ $116K + $22K + $10K ≈ ~$148K/month for ~20 hosts.
• Per request at ~10 rps average = ~26M/month. Cost ≈ $148K / 26M ≈ $0.0057/request ≈ $5.7 
per 1,000 requests. [ILLUSTRATIVE][DERIVED] (worked example, not reference)
Mode 2 — cloud GPU instances (rent, scale to load). An 8×H100 on-demand instance ~$20/hr 
(canonical $2.50/GPU-hr × 8). Because the workload’s peak is 40 rps, the fleet must be able to burst to 
~20 instances, but the average load is ~5 hosts’ worth — so the honest cloud bill is the instance-hours 
actually required. That requires a load-duration / capacity-occupancy model, not a single average. 
Parameterize the monthly cloud host-hours as Hcloud,month (host-hours, not Ch 17’s host-count N) — 
the integral of required capacity over time, plus the warm-pool and capacity-reservation terms:

193
TCO: The Total Cost Reality Check
Hcloud,month =
∫︂T
0
Nrequired(t) dt plus a warm/startup pool and any capacity-reservation guarantee
where Nrequired(t) is the number of hosts that must be running at time t (a step function that 
rises with the load curve at that hour of the day/week). This depends on the actual temporal load 
profile — the burst duration and frequency, the scale-up latency (how many seconds/minutes to 
attach and load a fresh instance), the model-loading/warmup time (loading a 140 GB model into a 
new host is not instant), the minimum warm pool to serve the base load without cold-starting, 
provider capacity/availability, and billing granularity. Only if the provider can attach instances 
faster than the load curve demands, with a warm pool already holding the model, can the cloud bill 
approach the pure average-load figure.
Under a simplified assumption of a continuously-arriving ~5-host average load that bursts 
briefly to ~20 at the peak, with a steady warm pool and fast scale-up, that is ≈ 5 × $20/hr × 
730 hr ≈ ~$73K/month (scaling the fleet with load; always-on-at-peak would be ~$292K/month). 
[ILLUSTRATIVE][DERIVED] — this is explicitly an assumed load-duration curve and fast 
elastically-scalable capacity, not a measured or guaranteed cost. If the burst is long-lived or scale-up 
is slow, the real cloud cost rises toward the always-on-at-peak figure and the “cloud is cheapest” 
result weakens accordingly.
The cloud case is a family, not a single line. $73K and $292K are the two endpoints (ideal 
fast-scale vs always-on-at-peak). The realistic midpoint is set by two parameters — the hours-at-
peak per day/week and the reserved-warm-instance count held to avoid cold-start. For example, a 
mid-case with ~5 reserved warm hosts + ~15 burst hosts held for ~4 peak hours/day, at $20/hr (burst) 
and the warm pool at a reserved rate, lands between the two endpoints; the exact number depends 
on the provider’s reserved/discount and billing-granularity terms, which are scenario inputs, not 
market facts. The durable lesson — fleet size first, price second — does not depend on this elasticity 
assumption, but the specific $2.8/1K number does and should be read as the fast-scale ideal, not the 
default decision line.
• Per request: ~26M/month. Cost ≈ $73K / 26M ≈ $0.0028/request ≈ $2.8 per 1,000 requests. 
[ILLUSTRATIVE][DERIVED] (The “duty cycle” here is fleet capacity utilization — how many 
of the provisioned hosts are busy on average — NOT the literal fraction of wall-clock time an 
instance is powered on. A continuously-arriving 10-rps workload cannot be served by shutting the 
only instance off 60% of the time; it needs a fleet that is always available and scales with load. 
Staff & integration add on top.)
Mode 3 — managed inference API.
• A hosted frontier-70B-class API at ~$0.002/input + ~$0.008/output per 1K tokens (typical as of 
2026). The per-request token cost is

Reading the result. Once the fleet is sized correctly (~20 hosts), the comparison flips entirely 
versus the single-host framing. Self-hosted (~$5.7/1K reqs) is now roughly twice the cloud-scaled 
rate ($2.8/1K) on a fully-loaded basis ($148K/mo vs ~$73K/mo), because the canonical workload 
genuinely needs a multi-host fleet, and self-hosting a 20-host fleet carries ~$116K/mo of capex 
amortization. It is nonetheless still well below the managed-API rate ($20.8/1K), so at this volume 
self-hosting beats the API but loses to scale-to-load cloud. This is an illustrative lower-bound for 
cloud, NOT an apples-to-apples comparison — the $73K cloud figure assumes ideal scale-to-load 
elasticity and a continuously-arriving ~5-host average that bursts briefly to ~20, whereas the $148K 
self-host figure is an always-provisioned 20-host fleet. They do not share the same availability ceiling, 
failover reserve, warm-capacity cost, peak-duration assumption, or p95 SLO. Cloud-scaled being 
cheapest is therefore a lower-bound result of an asymmetric scenario, not a demonstrated 
mode ranking. Without a measured traffic trace and equalized SLO/failover/availability assumptions 
on both sides, the definitive economic winner is not determined.
Apples-to-apples caveat. The $73K cloud figure does not yet include an N−1 failover reserve, 
capacity held for failures/maintenance, warm-capacity cost during idle periods, model-startup time, 
or provider availability variance; the self-host figure does not charge for the same availability target’s 
redundancy. So the two are not compared on equal reliability or load-serving assumptions. 
Before treating cloud as cheaper, add: the same served-load trace, p95 SLO, peak duration, availability 
target, failover coverage, warm capacity, model startup time, and price period to both sides. Under a 
nonideal elastic case (a reserved warm pool sized for N−1 plus slower scale-up), the cloud cost rises 
toward the always-on-at-peak figure and the “cloud is cheapest” result may not hold. As above: the 
$2.8/1K figure is an optimistic lower bound — frame the decision as “cloud is cheaper if scale-up 
is fast and a warm pool can be held cheaply,” not as an unconditional ranking. The decisive caveat 
remains the staff/ops term and whether it is sunk, and now also the fleet requirement — a 
single-host TCO understates the real cost by 5–20×.
Multi-region active-active availability is NOT priced in this TCO. The canonical workload’s 
stated SLA (Ch 4) is multi-region active-active at 99.9% availability, but every $/1K figure in this 
chapter (and the host counts in Ch 17) prices a single-region deployment with N−1 single-host 
failover, not multi-region active-active. Multi-region active-active is a different provisioning 
problem from N−1 single-host failover: it requires cross-region capacity duplication (no single 
region’s loss may take the service down), region-failure tolerance, data locality and traffic routing 
(geo/health-based routing, per-region caches and indices), and consistent state/data replication (model 

195
TCO: The Total Cost Reality Check
weights + KV/index replication across regions). None of that is priced or validated here, and none of 
it is implied by the N−1 reserve host above. Accordingly the canonical cost figures understate the 
cost of the canonical workload’s own stated 99.9% availability requirement — read the $/1K 
and monthly totals as single-region, N−1-only numbers, and add a separate multi-region capacity + 
replication cost line (on the order of ≥2× the single-region fleet on the capacity-duplication term 
alone) before the availability SLA can be considered met.

independent of KV size and still applies if the host is compute/bandwidth-bound — so the 
true fleet is max(N_throughput, N_KV), the larger of the two, not the KV bound alone. The 
full-MHA number is the conservative teaching case; own the 6–7× gap explicitly rather than quoting 
~20 hosts for a GQA deployment.)

Parametric sensitivity is the durable form. All the fixed inputs above — GPU price, electricity, 

TCO: The Total Cost Reality Check
198
staffing, capacity, API price, and the fleet size itself — are [ILLUSTRATIVE] scenario values; the 
structure of the model is what generalizes. The architect computes N_hosts from the workload 
first (Ch 17/20), then re-runs this TCO with the customer’s real numbers (Chapter 23). A runnable, 
parameterised version ships with the book as render/tco_calc.py.
Crucially, these are independent sensitivity dimensions, and each pushes the break-even in a 
different direction; do not collapse them into one “the assumptions vary” disclaimer:
• Fleet size (N_hosts) — driven by the KV/throughput/SLO ceiling (Ch 17-20), not by TCO. A 
larger fleet raises the flat self-host line (more capex+opex) and shifts break-even right.
• Duty cycle — a burst-sized fleet does not run every host at full paid duty all month. Self-
hosted reserved cards bill flat regardless of utilization; cloud on-demand bills only what runs. 
Separating these two is what makes the self-host (high fixed, low variable) vs cloud (low fixed, 
high variable) comparison meaningful.
• Utilization — the provisioning utilization target (70% vs 100%, Ch 17) sets how many hosts are 
needed, not how much they cost per unit; it is a separate line from the price-per-host.
• Reserved / on-demand mix — whether capacity is committed (cheaper, flat) or burst (pricier, 
on-demand) is a distinct lever; a mixed fleet has both a flat and a variable term.
• Failover reserve — N-1/headroom hosts (Ch 17) add cost but are not production-serving 
capacity; they must be counted as a separate reserve line, not folded into the served-load hosts.
• Staffing — the dominant human term for self-hosting; it is frequently the decider at low 
volume (Ch16 §16.8 mini-case) and should be its own sensitivity dimension, not absorbed into 
opex.
Because these dimensions are independent, the break-even is a region, not a point: the figure 
marks the [ILLUSTRATIVE] price band, and the honest output is a range (here ~5.9–9.9M req/mo) 
rather than a single “the break-even is 7.1M” claim. The headline range varies the API price against 
the full-utilization self-host cost ($148K/mo): the low end (~5.9M) is 148,000 ÷ 0.025 (the $25/1K top 
of the band) and the high end (~9.9M) is 148,000 ÷ 0.015 (the $15/1K bottom of the band) — the same 
~5.9–9.9M range reported with Fig 16.1 and in §16.6. The 70%-utilization provisioning case is 
a separate dimension and is named separately: the $217K/mo self-host cost against the canonical 
$20.8/1K API rate gives 217,000 ÷ 0.0208 ≈ 10.4M req/mo. Report the range and name which dimension 
is being varied.
16.4. Measurement
Four metrics keep the TCO model honest:
1. Cost per useful (SLO-meeting) request — the decision number; divide total cost by goodput-
meeting requests (Ch6), not raw tokens.
2. Effective utilization — actual goodput ÷ theoretical capacity across the billing period; low 
utilization is the hidden tax in both capex and rented instances.

199
TCO: The Total Cost Reality Check
3. Break-even volume — the requests/month where self-host cost-per-request crosses managed 
API; below it, don’t buy cards.
4. Staff/duty-cycle adjusters — the engineering-hours and duty-cycle assumptions that domi­
nate the comparisons; measure them, don’t assume.
16.5. Common Mistakes
• Comparing sticker price to per-token rate. A $/1K-token API quote already includes 
amortization; a card price tag does not. Amortize both.
• Ignoring the staff/ops term. Self-host “saves money” on paper and loses the moment two 
engineers are consumed full-time.
• Assuming 100% utilization. Idle cards are still burning electricity and depreciation; bake 
duty cycle in.
• Forgetting opportunity cost. A 6-month procurement cycle versus a same-week API inte­
gration is a real cost measured in time-to-value.
• Using peak capacity for cost-per-request. Peak sizing (40 rps) overstates cost if average 
(10 rps) utilization is what governs. (The headline $5.7/1K above does divide the peak-sized 
20-host fleet by average volume — deliberately: this workload must clear the 40 rps peak, so 
peak capacity is real cost here; the mistake applies when peak sizing is avoidable, e.g. batch or 
queueable traffic.)
16.6. Architecture Consequence
TCO is the final gate in the design loop (Ch12 candidates → Ch14 benchmark → Ch16 cost). It 
settles which candidate survives: the 70B 8×H100 self-host passes the capability screen and is 
analytically screened for feasibility — it would need a representative hardware benchmark 
to establish SLO-goodput (the C/W and roofline estimates of Chs. 7–8 are planning bounds, not 
a measured p95 TTFT/TPOT pass at the target offered load), and on TCO it beats the managed 
API at canonical volume but loses to scale-to-load cloud — so the surviving recommendation 
is conditional (self-host wins only if the staff/ops term is sunk and sustained near-peak utilization 
holds and a benchmark confirms the SLO; otherwise scale-to-load cloud at ~$2.8/1K is cheapest). This 
completes the chain from vague requirement to a defensible, costed architecture, while being honest 
that the TCO gate is not an unconditional win and that the SLO pass is provisional until benchmarked. 
It also feeds fleet decisions (Ch17-18): the break-even curve is what justifies buying a second host 
versus renting peaks, and the staff term is what pushes toward consolidation (Ch18).

• Real equipment/power/staff numbers for a given deployment are site-specific

• How rapidly GPU list and amortization prices fall over the 5-year horizon is

16.8. End-of-Chapter Mini-Case: TCO Discipline and the Staff Term
A startup’s CTO shows the architect a warrant to buy two 8×H100 servers for the new internal RAG 
assistant, citing “we’ll save on token fees.” Applying TCO discipline, the architect does not dispute 
the raw token math. Instead they price it: at this startup’s actual early volume — ~2 rps average, 
~5M requests/month, and only 0.5 engineers fully available for GPU ops — the break-even against 
a managed API is this chapter’s own division: $40,000 ÷ $0.0208 ≈ 1.9M requests/month, so the 
current volume is already ~2.6× past the break-even. The honest number: self-hosting two servers 
~$40K/month all-in vs ~$104K/month API at current volume (5M × $0.0208) — a ~$64K/month 
saving in favor of self-hosting, with the staff term already inside the $40K (capex ~$11.6K + 
power ~$2.2K for two hosts leaves ≤ ~$26K for staff — upkeep is the power line already counted, so 
~$0.2K of the $26.2K remainder is unallocated — too small to dwarf a $64K saving). The staff term 
is on a different basis than Mode 1: the mini-case prices a startup’s 0.5-FTE operator (~$26K/mo — 
≈$624K/yr fully loaded for one engineer, a scenario value rather than a market rate), not Mode 1’s 
mature-operator alloc (~$10K/mo for 20 hosts) — re-price on one staffing basis before comparing. 
The token arithmetic therefore does not send this startup to the API; it points to self-hosting. Only 
one term can flip the decision: the opportunity cost of the GPU-ops time the two servers consume 
— the better part of the only engineer the startup can spare. To justify paying the API premium, 
that term must exceed ~$64K/month, about $770K/year of product work the startup is not doing. 
The architect’s verdict: start on the managed API now, and revisit self-host the moment the 
consumed engineer’s opportunity cost falls below ~$64K/month — for example when a 
second engineer frees up — then re-run the same model. Request volume is not the lever: more 
volume only widens the ~$64K gap. The CTO did not buy the servers. The break-even graph did 
not make the answer self-evident; it narrowed the whole decision to one labeled assumption — this 
startup values its half-engineer’s product time above $64K/month. A team that cannot say that, or 
that has a second engineer to spare, runs the same numbers and buys the servers. 

part V.
The Fleet
202

chapter 17.
From Server to Fleet
The Architect’s Question
Having established the single-host serving model — a 70B FP16 model on 8×H100 serving the 
canonical workload (Ch 4, Table 4-2) at ~10 rps average / ~40 rps peak — the natural next question 
is: when does one host cease to suffice, and how do we scale to a fleet? This chapter answers the 
architect’s question: at what point must we move from a lone server to a coordinated fleet, and what 
are the quantitative trade-offs of that transition?
17.1. Concept
The transition from a single-server deployment to a fleet is not a qualitative leap but a quantitative 
threshold crossing. In the single-server model, all request routing, load balancing, and health moni­
toring occur within one process on one machine. The architect thinks in terms of request-per-second 
capacity, GPU memory utilization, and inference latency on that one host. As traffic grows, the single 
host hits limits in three domains: computational throughput (tokens/sec), GPU memory capacity, and 
inter-request interference (contention for shared resources such as NVMe I/O, PCIe bandwidth, and 
CPU). When any of these limits is breached, the fleet model introduces multiple identical hosts behind 
a load balancer, each independently serving a slice of the total traffic. The fleet model adds three 
new architectural ingredients: (a) a load balancer that distributes requests across hosts, (b) cross-host 
communication patterns when a request spans multiple machines, and (c) capacity planning that 
accounts for redundancy, failover, and the diminishing returns of horizontal scaling. The key insight 
is that horizontal scaling transforms per-host limits into system-wide capacities, but at the cost of 
added coordination overhead, state synchronization, and network budgeting.
203

From Server to Fleet

residual (~6.0 req/s) still clears it. Scope: the burst is a small illustrative example; the canonical 40 
rps peak is sized the same way to ≈20 hosts (Ch. 17-20). Queueing-model note: the ~2.0 req/s/host 
is a service-rate (capacity) bound, not a queueing result, and N-1 failover is a capacity argument, not 
a proof of p99 TTFT/TPOT — tail latency must be measured (Ch 14).
17.2. Mental Model
We visualize the fleet as a two-level hierarchy. At the top level, a load balancer — either hardware 
(L7 switch) or software (NGINX, HAProxy, Cloud Load Balancer) — accepts inbound requests and 
routes them to one of N backend hosts. Each backend host runs an identical copy of the model and 
serves requests independently. At the bottom level, each host operates exactly as the single-server 
model does: a model engine accepting prompts, producing completions, and returning tokens to the 
load balancer, which forwards the response to the client.
The mental model introduces two new invariants. First, the throughput invariant: total system 
throughput ≈ N × throughput_per_host, reduced by a factor ρ that captures load‑balancer over­
head, health‑check traffic, and imperfect distribution (typically ρ ∈ [0.85, 0.98] depending on LB 
sophistication). Second, the memory invariant: total GPU memory across the fleet is N × memory_
per_host, but the per-host memory available for model weights and KV cache remains the same as 
the single-host case. The fleet does not increase the per-host model size; it increases the aggregate 
number of concurrent requests the system can serve by parallelizing across hosts.
A critical detail is the session-affinity decision. If user sessions must maintain conversation 

205
From Server to Fleet
state (KV cache, accumulated tool outputs, retrieval context), the load balancer must route subsequent 
requests from the same user to the same host (sticky sessions). This reduces cross-host communication 
but limits the effective parallelism, because a popular user can pin a single host to capacity while other 
hosts stand underutilized. If sessions are stateless (the model is reloaded or KV cache is discarded 
between requests), any host can serve any request, and load distribution becomes even. The architect 
must choose between density (sticky sessions, higher per-host utilization) and evenness (stateless 
routing, lower per-host utilization but better load balance).
17.3. Worked Example
We continue from the canonical scenario (Ch 4, Table 4-2): ~10 rps average / ~40 rps peak, prompt 
~9,200 input tokens + ~300 output, 70B-class dense FP16 model, 8×H100 host with 640 GB GPU 
memory. A single 8×H100 host’s capacity is set by the binding constraint among GPU compute, 
HBM bandwidth, and KV cache memory. Let us verify with arithmetic, keeping the two quantities 
distinct: aggregate HBM (640 GB) is not the same as KV-available memory (640 − 140 GB weights 
− runtime/workspace reserve).
Single-host concurrency capacity (KV-bound). The per-token KV footprint for the canonical 
full-MHA reference geometry is 2 × layers × hidden × bytes = 2 ×80 ×8,192 ×2 = 2,621,440 B ≈ 2.62 
MB/token (Chapter 7 canonical). Subtract the 140 GB FP16 weights and ~64 GB runtime/activa­
tions/workspace from the 640 GB pool, leaving ≈436 GB available for KV. Each request at 9,200 input 
+ 300 output reserves a 9,500-token max KV of 9,500 ×2.62 MB ≈ 24.9 GB (the initial 9.2K residency 
is 24.1 GB). A single host therefore holds C = 436 GB ÷ 24.9037 GB ≈ 17.5 concurrent requests 
(byte-accurate; the conservative integer “requests that fit” floor is 17, not 18 — 17.51 cannot round 
up for a hard fit) — using the canonical layer-complete κ. Computing κ from a single layer instead of 
all 80 would overstate the concurrency figure by two orders of magnitude.
Viability at peak. Peak traffic is 40 rps; with the canonical ~8.6 s per-request service time (1.08 
s prefill + 7.5 s decode), Little’s Law puts ≈ 40 × 8.6 ≈ 344 requests in flight, against a KV capacity 
of ~17.5 per host (conservative integer floor 17). A single host is not viable at peak even for the 
non-agentic canonical workload — the fleet needs roughly ⌈344/17.5⌉ ≈ 20 hosts at 40 rps (29 
working hosts at the 70% utilization target, 30 total with N−1), and more for growth to 100 rps. 
This flips the original conclusion: the fleet is not a distant luxury but a necessity at the canonical 
peak, driven by the product of service time (8.6 s) and arrival rate.
KV compression is the first lever. Switching KV to FP8 (κ ≈ 1.31 MB/token, raw byte-halving 
— exactly 50% of BF16 [ILLUSTRATIVE][DERIVED]) cuts the 9.5K max per-request KV to ≈12.45 
GB and raises C to ≈35.0 concurrent per host (436 ÷ 12.45 ≈ 35.0; conservative integer floor 35), 
roughly doubling headroom before buying hardware. This is why the architect treats KV precision 
as a first-class capacity dial, not a cosmetic detail.
Threshold for fleet expansion. Suppose the agentic layer from Chapter 19 is added. Chapter 
19’s own telemetry puts the turn distribution at 68% terminating in T=1, 25% in T=2, 5% in T=3, and 

From Server to Fleet
206
2% exceeding the turn limit (falling back to single-shot). That makes the median turn count T=1 
(68% of requests finish on the first turn) and the 95th percentile T=3 (cumulative 68+25+5 = 98% 
by T=3). Each turn adds ~800 tokens of retrieved context (delta) and ~65 tokens of model-generated 
reasoning (gamma), so the effective per-request token count is
I(T) = I0 + T(δ + γ) + Ofinal = 9,200 + T × 865 + 300
• Median (T=1): I(1) = 9,200 + 1 × 800 + 1 × 65 + 300 = 10,365 tokens
• 95th percentile (T=3): I(3) = 9,200 + 3 × 800 + 3 × 65 + 300 = 12,095 tokens
• Hypothetical extension (T=4, if the turn limit were not enforced — per Ch19 the >2% that exceed 
the limit fall back to single-shot, so no request actually reaches T=4): I(4) = 9,200 + 4 × 800 +
4 × 65 + 300 = 12,960 tokens
The KV cache per request rises proportionally, and per-host concurrency capacity follows from 
the Ch17 formula C = Mkv/(I(T)κ):
• at T=1 (median): 10,365 × 2.62 MB ≈27.2 GB/request ⇒C ≈16 concurrent (floor 16)
• at T=3 (95th): 12,095 × 2.62 MB ≈31.7 GB/request ⇒C ≈13.8 concurrent (floor 13)
• at T=4 (hypothetical extension, turn limit not enforced): 12,960 × 2.62 MB
≈
34.0 GB/request ⇒C ≈12.8 concurrent (floor 12)
Even the median agentic request already drops a single host below the peak in-flight demand (C 
≈ 16 < 344 at 40 rps, or 860 at 100 rps), so the fleet is required under almost any agentic assumption. 
Scaling forecast (service-time-aware). Because the canonical workload’s service time is ~8.6 s 
— not a ~1 s latency — the in-flight concurrency at the 40 rps peak is λ_peak × W ≈ 40 × 8.6 ≈ 344 
requests, not 40. With FP16 KV (baseline C≈17.5, conservative integer floor 17), the fleet needs 
⌈344/17.5⌉ ≈ 20 hosts at full utilization (29 at the 70% utilization target). Agentic turns make this 
strictly worse: each additional turn adds ~800 tokens of context (lowering C) and more generated 
tokens (raising W), so the agentic T=3 case needs a larger fleet still (C≈13.8 in the 95th-percentile 
case and a longer W). The load balancer distributes λ/N rps per host and λ·W/N concurrent per host, 
and each host’s per-host KV usage must stay comfortably under its C. This is the fleet point: hosts 
are added because concurrency in flight × KV-per-request exceeds a single host’s residency — and 
concurrency in flight must be computed from the full service time, not a target latency.
Cross-host communication. In the canonical scenario, requests are independent — no user 
session spans multiple hosts. If an agentic loop required retrieving results from a prior host (e.g., 
distributed retrieval), the system would need a coordination layer (Redis, gRPC, or message queue) 
with added latency. We quantify this below in the measurement section.

From Server to Fleet
17.4. Measurement
We derive three real quantities from the worked example and from production traffic patterns.
17.4.1. Per-host throughput and KV cache budget
Let N be the number of hosts, λ the incoming rps peak, T the random variable for agent turn count 
with distribution P(T), δ = 800 tokens per turn of retrieved context, γ = 65 tokens of model-generated 
reasoning per turn, and O_final = 300 final output tokens. The per-request token count is
I(T) = I0 + T · δ + T · γ + Ofinal
where I₀ = 9,200 is the baseline input tokens. The per-host concurrent‑request capacity in terms 
of KV cache is
C =
Mkv
I(T) · κ
where M_kv is the KV‑available memory — the portion of on‑host HBM left for KV after weights 
and runtime — and κ = 2 × nlayers × dhidden × bytes ≈2.62 MB/token (2.5 MiB) is the per‑token 
KV footprint (Chapter 7 canonical; κ includes all layers — omitting the layer factor understates KV 
memory by orders of magnitude). For the canonical host, M_kv = 640 GB − 140 GB (FP16 weights) 
− ~64 GB (runtime/activations/workspace/NCCL) ≈ 436 GB. Dividing memory by bytes (not the 
byte‑divided‑by‑bytes collapse that trips unit analysis):
• For T = 0 (baseline, I = 9,500): C = 436 GB / (9,500 ×2.62 MB) ≈ 436/24.9037 ≈ 17.5 concurrent 
requests per host (byte-accurate; the conservative integer “requests that fit” floor is 17, not 
18 — 17.51 cannot round up for a hard fit).
• For T = 1 (median, per the Chapter 19 distribution; 68% terminate on turn 1): C = 436 GB / 
(10,365 ×2.62 MB) ≈ 436/27.17 ≈ 16 concurrent requests per host.
• For T = 3 (95th percentile; cumulative 98% by T=3): C = 436 GB / (12,095 ×2.62 MB) ≈ 436/31.71 
≈ 13.8 concurrent requests per host (floor 13).
• For T = 4 (hypothetical extension beyond the enforced turn limit — Ch19’s >2% tail falls back 
to single-shot, so no request reaches T=4): C = 436 GB / (12,960 ×2.62 MB) ≈ 436/33.97 ≈ 12.8 
concurrent requests per host (floor 12).
These numbers are the KV-residency ceiling; measured concurrency may be lower if GPU 
throughput or bandwidth binds first, so the architect takes the min of the KV, compute, and bandwidth 
capacities.
Reconciliation box — the four throughput rates in this book are different quan­
tities, not a contradiction. (1) ~50 tok/s (Ch. 5) is a deliberately conservative single-
stream, low-batch generation rate used to illustrate the tokens-per-dollar method; it is 
a floor, not the host’s serving throughput. (2) ~19,800 tok/s (Ch. 8) is the idealized 

From Server to Fleet
208
prefill aggregate (8 × 2,471 at 35% MFU) before TP/system loss — an analytical up­
per bound on prefill, not a servable goodput. (3) ~35,000–70,000 tok/s (Ch. 15) is the 
batched serving decode+prefill range under FlashAttention + continuous batching — 
the multi-request serving throughput of the same host. (4) ~2.0 req/s (Ch. 17/20) is the 
KV/service-time bound on whole requests per host (C ÷ W), the quantity that sizes the 
fleet. The first three are token-rates at different batch/phase assumptions; the fourth is a 
request-rate bound. They reconcile only phase-by-phase, never as one fungible “total” 
rate. Do not set a summed 19K tok/s against any single-phase capacity. At ~2.0 
req/s the request workload decomposes into two components that hit the hardware 
differently: 18,400 input tokens/s (2.0 × 9,200 — the prefill phase, compute-bound) 
and 600 output tokens/s (2.0 × 300 — the decode phase, bandwidth-bound) — 
both first-order regime labels; see Ch15/Ch8 for the scope conditions. Compare the 
prefill component only against the prefill-only capacities (~19,800 tok/s, item 2; ~35–70K 
only if those figures are themselves phase-resolved) and the decode component only 
against decode-scale capacity — never the summed ~19,000 tok/s against a single-phase 
figure. The apparent near-equality of ~19,000 tok/s and ~19,800 tok/s is a numerical 
coincidence of units, not a physical equivalence: the two sit in different phases with 
different arithmetic intensity and different cost (compute-bound prefill vs. bandwidth-
bound decode), so they cannot be added into one rate and compared to any single-phase 
number. Aggregate prefill + decode into a single “total” rate only after stating an explicit 
cost/phase-equivalence model (e.g., a weighted-FLOP or weighted-dollar basis). Use the 
token-rates for kernel and cost reasoning (keeping prefill and decode separate), and the 
request-rate for fleet sizing. [DERIVED]
