Sources, Method, and Reproducibility
This handbook marks every number with an evidence label so a reader never has to guess the 
provenance, the epistemic status, or the measurement status of a figure. The book publishes exactly 
one authoritative taxonomy, and it has three orthogonal axes: Axis 1 source provenance (where 
the number comes from), Axis 2 epistemic status (what the number claims to be), and Axis 3 
measurement status (how far the number has climbed the measurement-status hierarchy). Labels 
of the form [provenance][epistemic-status] combine one item from each of Axes 1 and 2; the 
Axis-3 measurement status rides with that pair (e.g. [LAB][MEASURED]) or stands alone (e.g. a bare 
[ANALYTICAL]). The axes are orthogonal — each answers a different question, so an item from one 
axis never substitutes for an item from another, and the measurement-status hierarchy below is Axis 
3 of this taxonomy, not a second, competing scheme.
Axis 1 — Source provenance: where the number comes from.
• [1P] First-party / primary source. A vendor technical report, model card, or official docu­
mentation. These are the model’s own stated parameters, benchmarks, and limits.
• [canonical scenario] The book’s own internally-defined canonical workload and model 
(Ch 4, Table 4-2). This is an internal source the book sets up so its arithmetic is anchored and 
reproducible; it is author-defined, not external first-party evidence. Used to distinguish ”where 
this number comes from inside the book” from ”[1P]” (a real vendor source).
• [2°] Secondary source. A third-party measurement, survey, or synthesis (e.g. an independent 
benchmark or a community write-up). Requires independent verification.
• [LAB] Measured on the author’s own hardware. These are home-lab measurements, not 
production hardware, and are used to illustrate mechanisms and orders of magnitude.
• [ILLUSTRATIVE] A worked-example or scenario input chosen to demonstrate a calculation, 
not a reported measurement. It is a number we assumed so the arithmetic would be concrete.
Axis 2 — Epistemic status: what the number claims to be.
• [FACT] Stated by the source as a fact (e.g. a parameter count or a vendor benchmark).
334

335
Sources, Method, and Reproducibility
• [DERIVED] Computed by arithmetic from stated inputs. The derivation is our work; the 
inputs come from a source item.
• [ASSUMPTION] A deliberately chosen scenario input (a number we picked so a worked 
example would be concrete), distinct from a hypothesis because it is not a claim about the real 
workload at all. It pairs with a provenance of [ILLUSTRATIVE]: [ILLUSTRATIVE][ASSUMP-
TION].
• [HYPOTHESIS] A reasoned expectation that has not yet been validated. Deliberately kept, 
because it distinguishes what we know from what we only expect.
• [UNRESOLVED] An open item that could not be pinned to a first-party source at the time 
of writing. Such items are flagged in the text and must be re-checked against current vendor 
documentation before any procurement or deployment decision; they are precisely the numbers 
most likely to have drifted. In evidence-label lines and copy-ready templates, the parenthetical 
“(to be verified)” is the plain-language form of [UNRESOLVED] and means exactly the same 
thing.
Axis 3 — Measurement status (the measurement-status hierarchy). Where Axis 1 says where 
a number came from and Axis 2 says what it claims to be, Axis 3 says how far it has climbed the 
measurement-status hierarchy — a third, orthogonal axis, folded into this one authoritative taxonomy 
rather than published as a separate scheme. A number in this book can fall into one of four stages, 
and they are not interchangeable. Reading “ceiling” as “achieved” is the single most consequential 
mistake an architect can make with these figures. The same convention is applied in the fleet and 
TCO synthesis tables (Chs. 12, 17, 20, 22).
Stage
What it is / what it is not
ANALYTICAL
an arithmetic or physical upper bound from stated inputs (e.g. KV 
occupancy ceiling C, or the per-host prefill rate). Valid for planning; 
not an achieved rate.
ILLUSTRATIVE
a worked-example value on stated assumptions, chosen to make the 
method concrete; not a measurement or a vendor claim.
MODELED (offered-load)
the result of a simulation or queueing model on a synthetic or 
recorded load curve; reflects the model, not a measured system.
MEASURED 
(SLO-goodput)
the only stage that demonstrates a deployed system meets a rate and 
a tail-latency SLO on real hardware. Requires a load test with a 
recorded trace.
In the text and figures, an [ANALYTICAL], [ILLUSTRATIVE], [MODELED], or [MEASURED] tag 
states the stage; where the stage is not yet determined, the value is marked [UNMEASURED] and 
cannot be treated as achieved throughput. A capacity ceiling becomes a deployment claim only after 
the [MEASURED] stage is reached. Author-run probes (a one-off API call to probe a capability) are 

Sources, Method, and Reproducibility
336
labeled [LAB] and are evidence of one functional path, not a model benchmark or an [1P] vendor 
claim; they never double as a vendor validation.
Why [ILLUSTRATIVE] appears on two axes. It is the same worked-example number viewed from 
two axes that answer different questions: on Axis 1 it is the provenance (an input we chose so the 
arithmetic would be concrete), on Axis 3 it is the stage (a worked-example value, not a measurement 
or a vendor claim). The two uses never conflict — a worked-example input is by definition at the 
ILLUSTRATIVE stage — so [ILLUSTRATIVE] is one token shared by two orthogonal axes, not an 
ambiguity.
Scope of the labeling. The label is mandatory for every load-bearing quantitative claim — a number 
that a later calculation, sizing decision, or comparison depends on; the Axis-3 stage rides with every 
load-bearing claim whose stage is known. Such a claim carries the labels directly (inline) or via an 
identified table or section that states them for every row. Purely illustrative or rhetorical numbers 
that no later calculation depends on (e.g. a round figure used only to make a sentence concrete) may 
carry the labels in the surrounding prose rather than inline. This is the unambiguous exception the 
preface refers to: the test is whether a downstream number depends on it.
Reading a label. [1P][FACT] means a vendor-stated fact; [1P][DERIVED] means arithmetic we 
computed from a first-party input; [LAB][FACT] means a home-lab measurement; [ILLUSTRA-
TIVE][ASSUMPTION] means a number we chose so the worked example would be concrete — it is 
not evidence of anything about the real workload. The Axis-3 measurement status reads alongside 
them: a bare [ANALYTICAL] means an arithmetic upper bound from stated inputs (valid for planning, 
not an achieved rate); [MODELED] means the result of a simulation or queueing model (it reflects 
the model, not a measured system); [MEASURED] means a load test with a recorded trace that 
demonstrates an achieved rate and tail-latency SLO on real hardware; [UNMEASURED] means the 
stage is not yet determined, so the value cannot be treated as achieved throughput; and the combined 
[LAB][MEASURED] pairs the Axis-1 provenance (our own hardware) with that Axis-3 stage. When 
only one axis is shown, the other is implied by the surrounding sentence (e.g. a bare [DERIVED] 
carries the status, with the provenance stated in prose).
The [S#] keys are a bibliography index, not a provenance. A label such as [S6][1P] combines 
a source reference key ([S6], pointing to an entry in the References chapter) with a provenance 
on that source ([1P]). The [S#] key is not a fifth provenance item; it merely says which reference 
the first-party or secondary evidence comes from. A composite label always carries at least one 
provenance item in addition to any [S#] key.
Prices, models, and benchmarks are a moving target. The model names, parameter counts, 
context limits, prices, and throughput figures in this book are a dated snapshot. They vary with 
the provider, region, commit/on-demand/spot status, hardware SKU, precision and sparsity mode, 
quantization, batch and concurrency, software stack, GPU count and topology, kernel version, warmup 
procedure, and percentile method used. Re-verify before acting.

337
Sources, Method, and Reproducibility
Reproducibility. The book’s quantitative examples are worked from the stated inputs in the sur­
rounding text; the home-lab measurements were made on the author’s own hardware under the 
configuration described in the chapter. Where a number is flagged as unresolved, the workspace did 
not have a first-party source at hand, and we say so rather than present an estimate as fact.
Home-lab scope. The author’s lab is a CPU-only workstation (Intel i5-3210M, four cores, 15 GB 
RAM). Measurements reflect that environment and the reduced-precision / small-model regime; they 
are used to illustrate mechanisms and orders of magnitude, not to characterize production hardware. 
Kernel-performance and throughput figures from public sources are to be treated as vendor-reported 
until re-measured on the target cluster. No H100/H200 production-performance claim in this 
book should be interpreted as a home-lab measurement unless explicitly labeled as such.
Code and data availability. The build scripts that produce this book (text → LaTeX → PDF, plus 
the figure generators) are in the accompanying repository. The figures are generated where possible 
(matplotlib and a hand-drawn diagram pipeline), so the arithmetic and layouts are inspectable rather 
than opaque artifacts.
Claim-to-source ledger. Every load-bearing number traces to a source by a fixed path, so a reader 
can re-derive it without guessing: (1) a [S#] key resolves to the matching entry in the References 
chapter, which gives the work, venue, and a stable identifier (arXiv id or first-party URL); all such 
sources were accessed as of the build date (v20260913). (2) a number derived from the canonical 
scenario traces to Ch 4, Table 4-2 (the single set of inputs every chapter cites). (3) the calculation 
itself is either the in-text arithmetic (stated with its operands) or the figure’s generator script in 
the repository (render/fig_*.py), which is reproducible from the stated inputs. A [1P] first-party 
claim is cited to its source but is a vendor statement, not an independent re-measurement; per the 
“moving target” note above, re-verify it against the current source before acting. This ledger is the 
mechanism; it does not substitute for the independent re-derivation and print proof that the release 
gates require.
Canonical parameter-and-unit ledger. Every derivative fleet/TCO/memory table cites this single 
ledger for its geometry, precision, and byte conventions, so a number is never silently re-based 
between the raw tensor overhead, the cache-block allocation, and the serving-engine observed 
footprint. The canonical scenario (Ch 4, Table 4-2) is full multi-head attention (MHA), 80 layers, 
hidden width 8192, BF16/FP16 KV, on an 8 × H100 host. The ledger is split into two blocks: the 
geometry and byte arithmetic and the planning and engine observations.

Reference-geometry convention. Any isolated per-token KV or memory figure in this book 
(e.g. ‘2.62 MB/token’, the raw FP8 ‘1.31 MB/token’, or a GB figure) refers to the canonical reference 
geometry — full multi-head attention (MHA), 80 layers, hidden width 8192, BF16/FP16 — unless the 
surrounding text or caption names a different geometry. This is the deliberately pessimistic upper 
bound from Ch. 1/7. It is not a typical 70B-model KV footprint: a real 70B model may use GQA, MLA, 
or hybrid attention, which reduce KV per token, so an architecture-specific figure must be derived 
for that model’s own attention shape rather than inheriting the MHA number. A decimal GB/binary 
GiB distinction is stated wherever a precise mount depends on it; where only an order of magnitude 
matters, the base is given explicitly.
Two conventions apply throughout. (1) “KV raw per token” always means the un-allocated array 
product, stated in base-2 (MiB) and base-10 (MB) as appropriate; “KV allocated” or “measured 
footprint” includes the metadata and cache-block overhead. Do not conflate them. (2) A precision 
adjective is always attached to its subject (“FP16 weights” vs “FP8 KV”), never implied. (3) An 
architecture-specific advantage (GQA ∼8×, MLA, hybrid) is a sensitivity for that configuration only 
and is not inherited by a different model.
Edition history. This is the release-information panel for the PDF produced from build v20260913. 
A later content change requires a new build identifier and a new row here — except a dated post-
cutoff addendum carried under its own revision identifier and listed in the Appendix addenda row; 
a re-render of the same source changes only the generation timestamp, not the identifiers. Any 
subsequent revision increments the build identifier and appends its change log here.

committed SHA plus each figure generator’s path hash (sha256 of 
render/fig_*.py at that commit), (3) the build command and 
environment (bash render/build.sh, Python/matplotlib versions), 
and (4) the final-file SHA-256, which is recorded in the external signed 
release manifest that accompanies the published PDF – not in the PDF’s 
own metadata, because a file’s digest cannot be stored inside that same 
file. All figures are built from committed inputs, and the post-cutoff 
addendum is separately labeled and is not folded into the 2026-08-30

Caption style convention. A figure caption follows a three-part order — (1) a one-sentence takeaway 
of what the figure shows, (2) a succinct ‘Scope/source:‘ line naming the geometry, assumptions, and 
evidence basis, and (3) any measurement warning — intended to make the figure scannable without 
deleting a qualification. A detailed derivation or cross-chapter reconciliation lives in the section 
body or a nearby note, not in the caption. Where a caption is observed to repeat a body paragraph at 
length, it is shortened to this convention rather than stripped of its provenance/scope condition.
Presentation of evidence tags. Inline provenance and status tags are carried on a number because a 
later calculation or decision depends on it (load-bearing). When the same tag applies to a contiguous 
run of related values or an entire section, the book does not repeat it on every line: it states the tag 
once in a boxed per-section Assumptions & evidence note (or in a table footnote that names the whole 
group), and the reader applies it to every value the note governs. A tag is always shown inline on a 
number that carries a different status from its neighbours, so the reader can spot a status change 
without re-reading the surrounding prose. Never strip a source/status control; group it, so it stays 
legible rather than fragmenting the sentence. 

The Architecture Worksheet
How to use this
Every chapter of this book turns a vague ask into a defensible number. This worksheet is the reverse: 
a single reusable worksheet that applies the whole loop to the architect’s own workload. Fill it in 
row by row; each row is a decision in Chapters 1–26. When a row is empty, that is precisely the gap 
to investigate next. The canonical workload box (Chapter 4) shows what a fully-filled row looks like; 
the two derived rates most often needed are given under its table.
All quantities are the architect’s own — traceable, verifiable, and stated in the evidence tax­
onomy (provenance: [1P] first-party, [canonical scenario] the book’s own workload, [2◦] reputable 
secondary, [LAB] home-lab, [ILLUSTRATIVE] worked-example; epistemic status: FACT / DERIVED / 
ASSUMPTION / HYPOTHESIS / UNRESOLVED; measurement status — the third, orthogonal axis 
— ANALYTICAL / ILLUSTRATIVE / MODELED / MEASURED / UNMEASURED). See the Sources, 
Method, and Reproducibility chapter for the full three-axis definition.

Following up
Keep one sheet per significant architecture decision, and re-check it when the workload’s numbers 
change. A machine-readable (Markdown) companion to this worksheet ships in the repository 
(render/architecture_worksheet.md), so a practitioner can copy, fill, and version it alongside 
the corresponding ADR rather than transcribing the printed page. The worksheet’s sequence — 
requirement, workload, model, compute/memory, architecture, decision, validation & operations — 
is the same loop Chapter 22 makes explicit, and the same reason the Patterns Library in Chapter 26 
exists: a decision recorded with its evidence can be reused, while a decision recorded without it must 
be re-derived every time. Rows 7–8 close the loop by pinning what was measured, at what load, the 
economics and failure policy assumed, and when it must be re-verified.
